========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hello, Dr. Lee. I'm here because... well, it’s a very complicated situation, and honestly, it’s been incredibly frustrating to deal with, but it all centers around this environmental modeling contract I’ve been managing. I work in mathematical sciences, you see—heavy dataset analysis, predictive algorithms, statistical modeling, that sort of thing. I’ve been doing this a long time, and I know my way around a database. 

But recently—and this has been going on for a while now, really—I’ve realized that the people on that specific environmental contract have been secretly tracking my revisions. Every single adjustment I make to the models, they are watching it. And it’s not just passive monitoring, either. They are actively going into the backend and planting errors in the datasets. They're altering the variables just enough to completely throw off the predictive outcomes, and they are doing it specifically to make it look like I can’t lead the work anymore. Like I'm incompetent or losing my edge. 

I try to explain this to the people around me, but no one wants to look at the actual math. My wife, for instance, she just brushes it off. She tells me I'm working too hard, and the junior analysts on my team—who barely have a fraction of the experience I do, mind you—they just roll their eyes and tell me it’s just version-control mistakes. They keep saying, "Oh, it's just a merge conflict," or "someone just pushed the wrong branch to the repository." But you don't get these highly specific, targeted mathematical anomalies from a simple version-control hiccup! It’s deliberate sabotage. They are tampering with the data to push me out, and it feels like I'm the only one who can actually see the patterns they're leaving behind.

Sad? Hopeless? No, no, no. I think you're misunderstanding the situation, Dr. Lee. I'm not sitting around crying or... or bouncing off the walls with some sort of unnatural energy. I mean, yes, I suppose you could say I’m irritable, but let’s put that in context, alright? If you spent six months building a multi-variable predictive model using decades of historical climate data—we're talking tens of thousands of rows of data here—and someone kept sneaking into the backend to subtly alter the floating-point values just to mess with your standard deviation... well, you would be irritable too! It’s a perfectly logical, rational response to professional sabotage. It is not a mood swing. 

My wife tries to do this too, you know. She sees me up at 2 AM checking the server logs, and she says, "Oh, you're not sleeping, you're so tense, are you feeling depressed?" And I have to explain to her over and over, I'm not depressed, and I'm not on some energetic high. I'm vigilant. I have to be awake to catch the timestamp anomalies because they usually do the tampering during off-peak server hours to avoid detection. So I'm just focused. I have a job to do and a reputation to protect. 

So, no, my mood is exactly what it should be for a man whose life's work is being actively dismantled by his own colleagues. It really frustrates me when people try to make this about my feelings instead of just looking at the math. If anyone would just sit down and look at the regression analysis from before and after they access the repository, they'd see I'm completely level-headed about this. It's all right there in the numbers, but no one wants to look at the numbers.

Well, you mentioned six months, and I did say I've been building the overarching model for six months, but... no, the actual tampering—the absolute certainty I have that they are actively tracking my revisions and deliberately planting these errors—that has been there for about the last two months. 

I can tell you exactly how it happened, too, because you don't just wake up one day and jump to the conclusion that your colleagues are sabotaging your life's work. It requires evidence. It was, let's see, a Tuesday, about eight or nine weeks ago. I was running a routine validation check on a subset of the atmospheric pressure data—just a standard procedural run, nothing out of the ordinary—and I noticed a variance that made absolutely no mathematical sense. I mean, we are talking about a deviation in the output that shouldn't exist in a dataset where the historical baseline hasn't fluctuated more than a fraction of a percent in three decades! 

So, naturally, I dug into the server logs. I spent, I don't know, maybe fourteen, fifteen hours straight tracing the commit history line by line. And that’s when I finally saw it. The timestamps. Someone had accessed my specific branch at 3:14 AM the night before and reverted a tiny, almost invisible cluster of floating-point variables. Just enough to poison the well, so to speak. 

And ever since that day—for the last two solid months—it has been a relentless, calculated campaign. Every time I fix the equations, every time I patch the dataset, they wait a few days, and then, boom, another anomaly appears right where I just worked. So, yes, two months of absolute, undeniable certainty. Before that? Well, maybe they were testing the waters, or maybe I just gave them the benefit of the doubt and thought it was sloppy data cleaning by the junior analysts. But for the last two months, the pattern is undeniable. If I could just show you the repository history, you'd see exactly what I mean.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Voices? Seeing things? Dr. Lee, I... I really think we are getting entirely off track here. I'm a mathematician. I deal in empirical data, in hard facts, in variables that can be plotted on a graph and verified by independent analysis. I don't... I don't hallucinate. I'm not sitting in my office listening to phantom whispers or seeing ghosts in the corner of the room. That's... that's just not what's happening. 

I understand why you have to ask these things—it's standard protocol, right? You have a checklist, you have to go down the list, rule out everything. My wife, she does the same kind of thing, jumping to these extreme conclusions just because I'm under a lot of stress. But no, absolutely not. I don't hear voices. The only sounds I hear are the hum of the server racks when I go down to the IT department to demand they audit the access logs, and the very real, very frustrating sound of my junior analysts making excuses for why the data keeps getting corrupted. 

And as for seeing things... the only things I'm seeing that other people aren't noticing are the patterns in the data. The mathematical anomalies. The deliberate, targeted alterations to the regression models. And it's not that they *can't* see them—it's that they refuse to look! If you pull up the standard deviation from the baseline model and compare it to the corrupted dataset, the tampering is right there in black and white. It's not a hallucination, it's a statistical impossibility! 

So, no. No voices, no visions, no imaginary friends. Just a very real, very documented campaign of professional sabotage that no one seems to want to take seriously. If we could just focus on the actual problem—the unauthorized access to my repository—I think we'd make a lot more progress. I actually brought a printout of the commit history, if you want to look at the timestamps yourself. It's fascinating, really, how they try to hide their tracks, but they always leave a digital footprint if you know where to look.

I'm vigilant. I have to be awake to catch the timestamp anomalies because they usually do the tampering during off-peak server hours to avoid detection. So I'm just focused. I have a job to do and a reputation to protect. \n\nSo, no, my mood is exactly what it should be for a man whose life's work is being actively dismantled by his own colleagues. It really frustrates me when people try to make this about my feelings instead of just looking at the math. If anyone would just sit down and look at the regression analysis from before and after they access the repository, they'd see I'm completely level-headed about this. It's all right there in the numbers, but no one wants to look at the numbers.\n\nDoctor: How long has it been since you first started noticing this tampering with your data—was it weeks, or has it been going on for six months or more?\n\nPatient: Well, you mentioned six months, and I did say I've been building the overarching model for six months, but... no, the actual tampering—the absolute certainty I have that they are actively tracking my revisions and deliberately planting these errors—that has been there for about the last two months. \n\nI can tell you exactly how it happened, too, because you don't just wake up one day and jump to the conclusion that your colleagues are sabotaging your life's work. It requires evidence. It was, let's see, a Tuesday, about eight or nine weeks ago. I was running a routine validation check on a subset of the atmospheric pressure data—just a standard procedural run, nothing out of the ordinary—and I noticed a variance that made absolutely no mathematical sense. I mean, we are talking about a deviation in the output that shouldn't exist in a dataset where the historical baseline hasn't fluctuated more than a fraction of a percent in three decades! \n\nSo, naturally, I dug into the server logs. I spent, I don't know, maybe fourteen, fifteen hours straight tracing the commit history line by line. And that’s when I finally saw it. The timestamps. Someone had accessed my specific branch at 3:14 AM the night before and reverted a tiny, almost invisible cluster of floating-point variables. Just enough to poison the well, so to speak. \n\nAnd ever since that day—for the last two solid months—it has been a relentless, calculated campaign. Every time I fix the equations, every time I patch the dataset, they wait a few days, and then, boom, another anomaly appears right where I just worked. So, yes, two months of absolute, undeniable certainty. Before that? Well, maybe they were testing the waters, or maybe I just gave them the benefit of the doubt and thought it was sloppy data cleaning by the junior analysts. But for the last two months, the pattern is undeniable. If I could just show you the repository history, you'd see exactly what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your certainty about the data tampering, have you ever heard voices, sounds, or seen things that other people around you don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Voices? Seeing things? Dr. Lee, I... I really think we are getting entirely off track here. I'm a mathematician. I deal in empirical data, in hard facts, in variables that can be plotted on a graph and verified by independent analysis. I don't... I don't hallucinate. I'm not sitting in my office listening to phantom whispers or seeing ghosts in the corner of the room. That's... that's just not what's happening. \n\nI understand why you have to ask these things—it's standard protocol, right? You have a checklist, you have to go down the list, rule out everything. My wife, she does the same kind of thing, jumping to these extreme conclusions just because I'm under a lot of stress. But no, absolutely not. I don't hear voices. The only sounds I hear are the hum of the server racks when I go down to the IT department to demand they audit the access logs, and the very real, very frustrating sound of my junior analysts making excuses for why the data keeps getting corrupted. \n\nAnd as for seeing things... the only things I'm seeing that other people aren't noticing are the patterns in the data. The mathematical anomalies. The deliberate, targeted alterations to the regression models. And it's not that they *can't* see them—it's that they refuse to look! If you pull up the standard deviation from the baseline model and compare it to the corrupted dataset, the tampering is right there in black and white. It's not a hallucination, it's a statistical impossibility! \n\nSo, no. No voices, no visions, no imaginary friends. Just a very real, very documented campaign of professional sabotage that no one seems to want to take seriously. If we could just focus on the actual problem—the unauthorized access to my repository—I think we'd make a lot more progress. I actually brought a printout of the commit history, if you want to look at the timestamps yourself. It's fascinating, really, how they try to hide their tracks, but they always leave a digital footprint if you know where to look.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, "keep up" is a very relative concept, Dr. Lee. If you're asking whether I'm checking off the standard, bureaucratic boxes that management wants on their timeline... then, well, no. I haven't been. But you have to understand the context of why that is. 

Because I've had to spend so many hours manually auditing those server logs and repairing the corrupted variables they keep planting, I’ve inevitably fallen far enough behind on the primary modeling deliverables that the firm... well, they made a very short-sighted decision. They actually reassigned pieces of the contract to another department. Just carved up my life's work and handed it off, completely ignoring the fact that I am the only one who truly understands the baseline architecture of that model! It's infuriating. And because I'm so consumed with trying to salvage the integrity of the data we still have, I’ve basically stopped being useful to the junior analysts I’m supposed to supervise. They come to my desk asking for help with some basic syntax error or a procedural question, and I just have to send them away. I simply don't have the bandwidth to hold their hands through basic tutorials when the entire project is being actively undermined from the inside. 

And as for my family... well, things at home are tense. I won't deny that. I mostly just keep to myself these days. I set up a secondary workstation in the spare room so I can monitor the repository without interruptions, and I just stay in there. My wife... she really doesn't understand the stakes of what's happening. She’s had to push me constantly just to keep basic routines going. She'll come in and interrupt my workflow to remind me to eat a meal, or take a shower, or she'll get upset that I haven't taken care of some household chore. It's just a constant stream of mundane distractions. 

I try

I have a job to do and a reputation to protect. \n\nSo, no, my mood is exactly what it should be for a man whose life's work is being actively dismantled by his own colleagues. It really frustrates me when people try to make this about my feelings instead of just looking at the math. That's... that's just not what's happening. \n\nI understand why you have to ask these things—it's standard protocol, right? You have a checklist, you have to go down the list, rule out everything. My wife, she does the same kind of thing, jumping to these extreme conclusions just because I'm under a lot of stress. But no, absolutely not. I don't hear voices. The only sounds I hear are the hum of the server racks when I go down to the IT department to demand they audit the access logs, and the very real, very frustrating sound of my junior analysts making excuses for why the data keeps getting corrupted. \n\nAnd as for seeing things... the only things I'm seeing that other people aren't noticing are the patterns in the data. The mathematical anomalies. The deliberate, targeted alterations to the regression models. And it's not that they *can't* see them—it's that they refuse to look! If you pull up the standard deviation from the baseline model and compare it to the corrupted dataset, the tampering is right there in black and white. It's not a hallucination, it's a statistical impossibility! \n\nSo, no. No voices, no visions, no imaginary friends. Just a very real, very documented campaign of professional sabotage that no one seems to want to take seriously. If we could just focus on the actual problem—the unauthorized access to my repository—I think we'd make a lot more progress. I actually brought a printout of the commit history, if you want to look at the timestamps yourself. It's fascinating, really, how they try to hide their tracks, but they always leave a digital footprint if you know where to look.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this conflict over the data, how have things been going otherwise—are you still able to keep up with your other work tasks, sleep, and get along with your family and coworkers as usual?\n\nPatient: Well, \"keep up\" is a very relative concept, Dr. Lee. If you're asking whether I'm checking off the standard, bureaucratic boxes that management wants on their timeline... then, well, no. I haven't been. But you have to understand the context of why that is. \n\nBecause I've had to spend so many hours manually auditing those server logs and repairing the corrupted variables they keep planting, I’ve inevitably fallen far enough behind on the primary modeling deliverables that the firm... well, they made a very short-sighted decision. They actually reassigned pieces of the contract to another department. Just carved up my life's work and handed it off, completely ignoring the fact that I am the only one who truly understands the baseline architecture of that model! It's infuriating. And because I'm so consumed with trying to salvage the integrity of the data we still have, I’ve basically stopped being useful to the junior analysts I’m supposed to supervise. They come to my desk asking for help with some basic syntax error or a procedural question, and I just have to send them away. I simply don't have the bandwidth to hold their hands through basic tutorials when the entire project is being actively undermined from the inside. \n\nAnd as for my family... well, things at home are tense. I won't deny that. I mostly just keep to myself these days. I set up a secondary workstation in the spare room so I can monitor the repository without interruptions, and I just stay in there. My wife... she really doesn't understand the stakes of what's happening. She’s had to push me constantly just to keep basic routines going. She'll come in and interrupt my workflow to remind me to eat a meal, or take a shower, or she'll get upset that I haven't taken care of some household chore. It's just a constant stream of mundane distractions. \n\nI try\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been using any medications, supplements, alcohol, or recreational drugs—like stimulants or caffeine pills—especially during those long nights monitoring the servers?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Medications? Alcohol? No, no, absolutely not. Dr. Lee, you have to understand the level of cognitive precision that is required to trace a microscopic floating-point anomaly through tens of thousands of lines of code. If I were taking stimulants, or, God forbid, drinking alcohol, my error rate would skyrocket, and my baseline concentration would be completely compromised. 

I need my mind to be sharp. Razor sharp. If I started altering my neurochemistry with recreational drugs or these... these supplements people take to supposedly "hack" their focus, I would be playing right into their hands. Can you imagine? They would love nothing more than for me to make a sloppy, careless mistake because my hands were jittery from some over-the-counter caffeine pill. Then they could just point to the server logs and tell management, "Look, look at these keystroke errors, he's the one corrupting the data, he's losing his edge!" It would be the perfect cover for their sabotage. 

So, no, I don't touch any of that. My wife actually asked me something similar a few weeks ago. She found a bottle of standard, generic ibuprofen on my desk and practically interrogated me about it, as if I were running some sort of illicit pharmacy out of the spare bedroom. I had to explain to her, very slowly, that staring at a backlit monitor for eighteen hours straight looking for unauthorized commits will occasionally give a person a tension headache! But that's it. Just ibuprofen for the strain on my eyes. 

When I'm up at three or four in the morning—which, again, I have to be, because that is the exact window when they usually try to push the corrupted variables to the main branch—I don't need chemical assistance to stay awake. The sheer adrenaline of knowing they are out there, actively trying to dismantle my life's work, is more than enough to keep me alert. I just sit there with a glass of ice water and watch the terminal. That's all I need. Just me, a clear head, and the raw data. Anything else would just be a liability.

If you spent six months building a multi-variable predictive model using decades of historical climate data—we're talking tens of thousands of rows of data here—and someone kept sneaking into the backend to subtly alter the floating-point values just to mess with your standard deviation... well, you would be irritable too! It’s a perfectly logical, rational response to professional sabotage. It is not a mood swing. \n\nMy wife tries to do this too, you know. She sees me up at 2 AM checking the server logs, and she says, \"Oh, you're not sleeping, you're so tense, are you feeling depressed?\" And I have to explain to her over and over, I'm not depressed, and I'm not on some energetic high. I'm vigilant. I have to be awake to catch the timestamp anomalies because they usually do the tampering during off-peak server hours to avoid detection. So I'm just focused. I have a job to do and a reputation to protect. \n\nSo, no, my mood is exactly what it should be for a man whose life's work is being actively dismantled by his own colleagues. It really frustrates me when people try to make this about my feelings instead of just looking at the math. If anyone would just sit down and look at the regression analysis from before and after they access the repository, they'd see I'm completely level-headed about this. It's all right there in the numbers, but no one wants to look at the numbers.\n\nDoctor: How long has it been since you first started noticing this tampering with your data—was it weeks, or has it been going on for six months or more?\n\nPatient: Well, you mentioned six months, and I did say I've been building the overarching model for six months, but... no, the actual tampering—the absolute certainty I have that they are actively tracking my revisions and deliberately planting these errors—that has been there for about the last two months. \n\nI can tell you exactly how it happened, too, because you don't just wake up one day and jump to the conclusion that your colleagues are sabotaging your life's work. It requires evidence. It was, let's see, a Tuesday, about eight or nine weeks ago. I was running a routine validation check on a subset of the atmospheric pressure data—just a standard procedural run, nothing out of the ordinary—and I noticed a variance that made absolutely no mathematical sense. I mean, we are talking about a deviation in the output that shouldn't exist in a dataset where the historical baseline hasn't fluctuated more than a fraction of a percent in three decades! \n\nSo, naturally, I dug into the server logs. I spent, I don't know, maybe fourteen, fifteen hours straight tracing the commit history line by line. And that’s when I finally saw it. The timestamps. Someone had accessed my specific branch at 3:14 AM the night before and reverted a tiny, almost invisible cluster of floating-point variables. Just enough to poison the well, so to speak. \n\nAnd ever since that day—for the last two solid months—it has been a relentless, calculated campaign. Every time I fix the equations, every time I patch the dataset, they wait a few days, and then, boom, another anomaly appears right where I just worked. So, yes, two months of absolute, undeniable certainty. Before that? Well, maybe they were testing the waters, or maybe I just gave them the benefit of the doubt and thought it was sloppy data cleaning by the junior analysts. But for the last two months, the pattern is undeniable. If I could just show you the repository history, you'd see exactly what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your certainty about the data tampering, have you ever heard voices, sounds, or seen things that other people around you don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Voices? Seeing things? Dr. Lee, I... I really think we are getting entirely off track here. I'm a mathematician. I deal in empirical data, in hard facts, in variables that can be plotted on a graph and verified by independent analysis. I don't... I don't hallucinate. I'm not sitting in my office listening to phantom whispers or seeing ghosts in the corner of the room. That's... that's just not what's happening. \n\nI understand why you have to ask these things—it's standard protocol, right? You have a checklist, you have to go down the list, rule out everything. My wife, she does the same kind of thing, jumping to these extreme conclusions just because I'm under a lot of stress. But no, absolutely not. I don't hear voices. The only sounds I hear are the hum of the server racks when I go down to the IT department to demand they audit the access logs, and the very real, very frustrating sound of my junior analysts making excuses for why the data keeps getting corrupted. \n\nAnd as for seeing things... the only things I'm seeing that other people aren't noticing are the patterns in the data. The mathematical anomalies. The deliberate, targeted alterations to the regression models. And it's not that they *can't* see them—it's that they refuse to look! If you pull up the standard deviation from the baseline model and compare it to the corrupted dataset, the tampering is right there in black and white. It's not a hallucination, it's a statistical impossibility! \n\nSo, no. No voices, no visions, no imaginary friends. Just a very real, very documented campaign of professional sabotage that no one seems to want to take seriously. If we could just focus on the actual problem—the unauthorized access to my repository—I think we'd make a lot more progress. I actually brought a printout of the commit history, if you want to look at the timestamps yourself. It's fascinating, really, how they try to hide their tracks, but they always leave a digital footprint if you know where to look.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this conflict over the data, how have things been going otherwise—are you still able to keep up with your other work tasks, sleep, and get along with your family and coworkers as usual?\n\nPatient: Well, \"keep up\" is a very relative concept, Dr. Lee. If you're asking whether I'm checking off the standard, bureaucratic boxes that management wants on their timeline... then, well, no. I haven't been. But you have to understand the context of why that is. \n\nBecause I've had to spend so many hours manually auditing those server logs and repairing the corrupted variables they keep planting, I’ve inevitably fallen far enough behind on the primary modeling deliverables that the firm... well, they made a very short-sighted decision. They actually reassigned pieces of the contract to another department. Just carved up my life's work and handed it off, completely ignoring the fact that I am the only one who truly understands the baseline architecture of that model! It's infuriating. And because I'm so consumed with trying to salvage the integrity of the data we still have, I’ve basically stopped being useful to the junior analysts I’m supposed to supervise. They come to my desk asking for help with some basic syntax error or a procedural question, and I just have to send them away. I simply don't have the bandwidth to hold their hands through basic tutorials when the entire project is being actively undermined from the inside. \n\nAnd as for my family... well, things at home are tense. I won't deny that. I mostly just keep to myself these days. I set up a secondary workstation in the spare room so I can monitor the repository without interruptions, and I just stay in there. My wife... she really doesn't understand the stakes of what's happening. She’s had to push me constantly just to keep basic routines going. She'll come in and interrupt my workflow to remind me to eat a meal, or take a shower, or she'll get upset that I haven't taken care of some household chore. It's just a constant stream of mundane distractions. \n\nI try\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any medications, supplements, alcohol, or recreational drugs—like stimulants or caffeine pills—especially during those long nights monitoring the servers?\n\nPatient: Medications? Alcohol? No, no, absolutely not. Dr. Lee, you have to understand the level of cognitive precision that is required to trace a microscopic floating-point anomaly through tens of thousands of lines of code. If I were taking stimulants, or, God forbid, drinking alcohol, my error rate would skyrocket, and my baseline concentration would be completely compromised. \n\nI need my mind to be sharp. Razor sharp. If I started altering my neurochemistry with recreational drugs or these... these supplements people take to supposedly \"hack\" their focus, I would be playing right into their hands. Can you imagine? They would love nothing more than for me to make a sloppy, careless mistake because my hands were jittery from some over-the-counter caffeine pill. Then they could just point to the server logs and tell management, \"Look, look at these keystroke errors, he's the one corrupting the data, he's losing his edge!\" It would be the perfect cover for their sabotage. \n\nSo, no, I don't touch any of that. My wife actually asked me something similar a few weeks ago. She found a bottle of standard, generic ibuprofen on my desk and practically interrogated me about it, as if I were running some sort of illicit pharmacy out of the spare bedroom. I had to explain to her, very slowly, that staring at a backlit monitor for eighteen hours straight looking for unauthorized commits will occasionally give a person a tension headache! But that's it. Just ibuprofen for the strain on my eyes. \n\nWhen I'm up at three or four in the morning—which, again, I have to be, because that is the exact window when they usually try to push the corrupted variables to the main branch—I don't need chemical assistance to stay awake. The sheer adrenaline of knowing they are out there, actively trying to dismantle my life's work, is more than enough to keep me alert. I just sit there with a glass of ice water and watch the terminal. That's all I need. Just me, a clear head, and the raw data. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Organize my thoughts? Dr. Lee, if anything, my thoughts have never been more meticulously organized. I mean... you don't track a multi-layered, systematic corruption of a predictive algorithm by having a disorganized mind. Every single time I sit down at that terminal, I have to mentally map out the entire architecture of the database before I even type a single command. I am holding complex arrays of variables in my head, cross-referencing timestamps, indexing the specific anomalies I documented the night before... that requires an immense, almost exhausting level of cognitive organization. So, no, I am certainly not having trouble thinking clearly. 

As for conversations... well, I suppose I don't have much patience for trivialities right now. If someone comes to me—like those junior analysts I mentioned—and they want to talk about some basic procedural issue that they should be able to resolve with a simple query, I might cut them off. I might walk away. But that's not because I can't follow what they're saying. It's because what they're saying is completely irrelevant to the crisis at hand! 

My wife, she does the same thing. She'll try to talk to me about... I don't know, the gutters needing cleaning, or some grocery list, right while I'm in the middle of compiling a critical report on the data breaches. And when I don't immediately drop everything to engage with her, she accuses me of being... what did she call it... "in my own world." But I'm not in my own world, Dr. Lee. I'm in the real world, dealing with real, quantifiable threats to my career, while everyone else is perfectly content to worry about mundane distractions. It's not that I can't follow a conversation, it's that I refuse to be derailed by pointless ones.

And these "unusual routines" you're asking about... I mean, what exactly constitutes unusual? Is it unusual to set an alarm for 3:15 AM because you've statistically mapped the highest probability of unauthorized server access to that exact time window? To a layman, sure, maybe that looks strange. Maybe eating a cold sandwich at my spare-room desk at 4:00 AM instead of sitting at the dining room table at 6:00 PM seems "unusual" to my wife. But it's not a symptom of anything. It's a perfectly rational adaptation to an irregular problem. If the people sabotaging my work operated on a standard nine-to-five schedule, I'd have a nine-to-five routine. But they don't. So I don't. It's simple cause and effect. Everything I do right now is entirely logical if you just look at the parameters of the situation I'm being forced to deal with.

I'm here because... well, it’s a very complicated situation, and honestly, it’s been incredibly frustrating to deal with, but it all centers around this environmental modeling contract I’ve been managing. I work in mathematical sciences, you see—heavy dataset analysis, predictive algorithms, statistical modeling, that sort of thing. I’ve been doing this a long time, and I know my way around a database. \n\nBut recently—and this has been going on for a while now, really—I’ve realized that the people on that specific environmental contract have been secretly tracking my revisions. Every single adjustment I make to the models, they are watching it. And it’s not just passive monitoring, either. They are actively going into the backend and planting errors in the datasets. They're altering the variables just enough to completely throw off the predictive outcomes, and they are doing it specifically to make it look like I can’t lead the work anymore. They are tampering with the data to push me out, and it feels like I'm the only one who can actually see the patterns they're leaving behind.\n\nDoctor: Alongside these concerns about sabotage, have you noticed any changes in your mood—like feeling unusually sad, hopeless, or on the other hand, unusually energetic, elated, or irritable?\n\nPatient: Sad? If you spent six months building a multi-variable predictive model using decades of historical climate data—we're talking tens of thousands of rows of data here—and someone kept sneaking into the backend to subtly alter the floating-point values just to mess with your standard deviation... well, you would be irritable too! It’s a perfectly logical, rational response to professional sabotage. It is not a mood swing. \n\nMy wife tries to do this too, you know. She sees me up at 2 AM checking the server logs, and she says, \"Oh, you're not sleeping, you're so tense, are you feeling depressed?\" And I have to explain to her over and over, I'm not depressed, and I'm not on some energetic high. I'm vigilant. I have to be awake to catch the timestamp anomalies because they usually do the tampering during off-peak server hours to avoid detection. So I'm just focused. I have a job to do and a reputation to protect. \n\nSo, no, my mood is exactly what it should be for a man whose life's work is being actively dismantled by his own colleagues. It really frustrates me when people try to make this about my feelings instead of just looking at the math. If anyone would just sit down and look at the regression analysis from before and after they access the repository, they'd see I'm completely level-headed about this. It's all right there in the numbers, but no one wants to look at the numbers.\n\nDoctor: How long has it been since you first started noticing this tampering with your data—was it weeks, or has it been going on for six months or more?\n\nPatient: Well, you mentioned six months, and I did say I've been building the overarching model for six months, but... no, the actual tampering—the absolute certainty I have that they are actively tracking my revisions and deliberately planting these errors—that has been there for about the last two months. \n\nI can tell you exactly how it happened, too, because you don't just wake up one day and jump to the conclusion that your colleagues are sabotaging your life's work. It requires evidence. It was, let's see, a Tuesday, about eight or nine weeks ago. I was running a routine validation check on a subset of the atmospheric pressure data—just a standard procedural run, nothing out of the ordinary—and I noticed a variance that made absolutely no mathematical sense. I mean, we are talking about a deviation in the output that shouldn't exist in a dataset where the historical baseline hasn't fluctuated more than a fraction of a percent in three decades! \n\nSo, naturally, I dug into the server logs. I spent, I don't know, maybe fourteen, fifteen hours straight tracing the commit history line by line. And that’s when I finally saw it. The timestamps. Someone had accessed my specific branch at 3:14 AM the night before and reverted a tiny, almost invisible cluster of floating-point variables. Just enough to poison the well, so to speak. \n\nAnd ever since that day—for the last two solid months—it has been a relentless, calculated campaign. Every time I fix the equations, every time I patch the dataset, they wait a few days, and then, boom, another anomaly appears right where I just worked. So, yes, two months of absolute, undeniable certainty. Before that? Well, maybe they were testing the waters, or maybe I just gave them the benefit of the doubt and thought it was sloppy data cleaning by the junior analysts. But for the last two months, the pattern is undeniable. If I could just show you the repository history, you'd see exactly what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your certainty about the data tampering, have you ever heard voices, sounds, or seen things that other people around you don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Voices? Seeing things? Dr. Lee, I... I really think we are getting entirely off track here. I'm a mathematician. I deal in empirical data, in hard facts, in variables that can be plotted on a graph and verified by independent analysis. I don't... I don't hallucinate. I'm not sitting in my office listening to phantom whispers or seeing ghosts in the corner of the room. That's... that's just not what's happening. \n\nI understand why you have to ask these things—it's standard protocol, right? You have a checklist, you have to go down the list, rule out everything. My wife, she does the same kind of thing, jumping to these extreme conclusions just because I'm under a lot of stress. But no, absolutely not. I don't hear voices. The only sounds I hear are the hum of the server racks when I go down to the IT department to demand they audit the access logs, and the very real, very frustrating sound of my junior analysts making excuses for why the data keeps getting corrupted. \n\nAnd as for seeing things... the only things I'm seeing that other people aren't noticing are the patterns in the data. The mathematical anomalies. The deliberate, targeted alterations to the regression models. And it's not that they *can't* see them—it's that they refuse to look! If you pull up the standard deviation from the baseline model and compare it to the corrupted dataset, the tampering is right there in black and white. It's not a hallucination, it's a statistical impossibility! \n\nSo, no. No voices, no visions, no imaginary friends. Just a very real, very documented campaign of professional sabotage that no one seems to want to take seriously. If we could just focus on the actual problem—the unauthorized access to my repository—I think we'd make a lot more progress. I actually brought a printout of the commit history, if you want to look at the timestamps yourself. It's fascinating, really, how they try to hide their tracks, but they always leave a digital footprint if you know where to look.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this conflict over the data, how have things been going otherwise—are you still able to keep up with your other work tasks, sleep, and get along with your family and coworkers as usual?\n\nPatient: Well, \"keep up\" is a very relative concept, Dr. Lee. If you're asking whether I'm checking off the standard, bureaucratic boxes that management wants on their timeline... then, well, no. I haven't been. But you have to understand the context of why that is. \n\nBecause I've had to spend so many hours manually auditing those server logs and repairing the corrupted variables they keep planting, I’ve inevitably fallen far enough behind on the primary modeling deliverables that the firm... well, they made a very short-sighted decision. They actually reassigned pieces of the contract to another department. Just carved up my life's work and handed it off, completely ignoring the fact that I am the only one who truly understands the baseline architecture of that model! It's infuriating. And because I'm so consumed with trying to salvage the integrity of the data we still have, I’ve basically stopped being useful to the junior analysts I’m supposed to supervise. They come to my desk asking for help with some basic syntax error or a procedural question, and I just have to send them away. I simply don't have the bandwidth to hold their hands through basic tutorials when the entire project is being actively undermined from the inside. \n\nAnd as for my family... well, things at home are tense. I won't deny that. I mostly just keep to myself these days. I set up a secondary workstation in the spare room so I can monitor the repository without interruptions, and I just stay in there. My wife... she really doesn't understand the stakes of what's happening. She’s had to push me constantly just to keep basic routines going. She'll come in and interrupt my workflow to remind me to eat a meal, or take a shower, or she'll get upset that I haven't taken care of some household chore. It's just a constant stream of mundane distractions. \n\nI try\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any medications, supplements, alcohol, or recreational drugs—like stimulants or caffeine pills—especially during those long nights monitoring the servers?\n\nPatient: Medications? Alcohol? No, no, absolutely not. Dr. Lee, you have to understand the level of cognitive precision that is required to trace a microscopic floating-point anomaly through tens of thousands of lines of code. If I were taking stimulants, or, God forbid, drinking alcohol, my error rate would skyrocket, and my baseline concentration would be completely compromised. \n\nI need my mind to be sharp. Razor sharp. If I started altering my neurochemistry with recreational drugs or these... these supplements people take to supposedly \"hack\" their focus, I would be playing right into their hands. Can you imagine? They would love nothing more than for me to make a sloppy, careless mistake because my hands were jittery from some over-the-counter caffeine pill. Then they could just point to the server logs and tell management, \"Look, look at these keystroke errors, he's the one corrupting the data, he's losing his edge!\" It would be the perfect cover for their sabotage. \n\nSo, no, I don't touch any of that. My wife actually asked me something similar a few weeks ago. She found a bottle of standard, generic ibuprofen on my desk and practically interrogated me about it, as if I were running some sort of illicit pharmacy out of the spare bedroom. I had to explain to her, very slowly, that staring at a backlit monitor for eighteen hours straight looking for unauthorized commits will occasionally give a person a tension headache! But that's it. Just ibuprofen for the strain on my eyes. \n\nWhen I'm up at three or four in the morning—which, again, I have to be, because that is the exact window when they usually try to push the corrupted variables to the main branch—I don't need chemical assistance to stay awake. The sheer adrenaline of knowing they are out there, actively trying to dismantle my life's work, is more than enough to keep me alert. I just sit there with a glass of ice water and watch the terminal. That's all I need. Just me, a clear head, and the raw data. Anything else would just be a liability.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your concerns about the data tampering, have you or others noticed any changes in how you organize your thoughts, speech, or daily behavior—such as trouble following a conversation or unusual routines?\n\nPatient: Organize my thoughts? Dr. Lee, if anything, my thoughts have never been more meticulously organized. I mean... you don't track a multi-layered, systematic corruption of a predictive algorithm by having a disorganized mind. Every single time I sit down at that terminal, I have to mentally map out the entire architecture of the database before I even type a single command. I am holding complex arrays of variables in my head, cross-referencing timestamps, indexing the specific anomalies I documented the night before... that requires an immense, almost exhausting level of cognitive organization. So, no, I am certainly not having trouble thinking clearly. \n\nAs for conversations... well, I suppose I don't have much patience for trivialities right now. If someone comes to me—like those junior analysts I mentioned—and they want to talk about some basic procedural issue that they should be able to resolve with a simple query, I might cut them off. I might walk away. But that's not because I can't follow what they're saying. It's because what they're saying is completely irrelevant to the crisis at hand! \n\nMy wife, she does the same thing. She'll try to talk to me about... I don't know, the gutters needing cleaning, or some grocery list, right while I'm in the middle of compiling a critical report on the data breaches. And when I don't immediately drop everything to engage with her, she accuses me of being... what did she call it... \"in my own world.\" But I'm not in my own world, Dr. Lee. I'm in the real world, dealing with real, quantifiable threats to my career, while everyone else is perfectly content to worry about mundane distractions. It's not that I can't follow a conversation, it's that I refuse to be derailed by pointless ones.\n\nAnd these \"unusual routines\" you're asking about... I mean, what exactly constitutes unusual? Is it unusual to set an alarm for 3:15 AM because you've statistically mapped the highest probability of unauthorized server access to that exact time window? To a layman, sure, maybe that looks strange. Maybe eating a cold sandwich at my spare-room desk at 4:00 AM instead of sitting at the dining room table at 6:00 PM seems \"unusual\" to my wife. But it's not a symptom of anything. It's a perfectly rational adaptation to an irregular problem. If the people sabotaging my work operated on a standard nine-to-five schedule, I'd have a nine-to-five routine. But they don't. So I don't. It's simple cause and effect. Everything I do right now is entirely logical if you just look at the parameters of the situation I'm being forced to deal with.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Just carved up my life's work and handed it off, completely ignoring the fact that I am the only one who truly understands the baseline architecture of that model! It's infuriating. And because I'm so consumed with trying to salvage the integrity of the data we still have, I’ve basically stopped being useful to the junior analysts I’m supposed to supervise. They come to my desk asking for help with some basic syntax error or a procedural question, and I just have to send them away. I simply don't have the bandwidth to hold their hands through basic tutorials when the entire project is being actively undermined from the inside. \n\nAnd as for my family... well, things at home are tense. I won't deny that. I mostly just keep to myself these days. I set up a secondary workstation in the spare room so I can monitor the repository without interruptions, and I just stay in there. She'll come in and interrupt my workflow to remind me to eat a meal, or take a shower, or she'll get upset that I haven't taken care of some household chore. It's just a constant stream of mundane distractions. \n\nI try\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any medications, supplements, alcohol, or recreational drugs—like stimulants or caffeine pills—especially during those long nights monitoring the servers?\n\nPatient: Medications? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
An independent IT security expert? Dr. Lee, I... I don't mean to sound condescending, I really don't, but you have to understand the sheer level of sophistication we are dealing with here. An outside IT expert would be completely useless. They would come in, they would run a standard diagnostic, they would look at the exact same surface-level logs that the junior analysts look at, and they would probably come to the exact same, completely flawed conclusion. 

You see, an external auditor is trained to look for external threats. They look for unauthorized access tokens, brute-force login attempts, malware signatures... basic, entry-level security breaches. But the people we are talking about already have internal clearance! They are inside the system. So an IT expert is just going to see valid credentials making modifications to a dataset. They don't know the complex mathematical architecture of my predictive algorithm. They don't have the decades of statistical modeling experience required to recognize that shifting a localized atmospheric weight by three decimal points isn't a typo, but a highly calculated structural attack. 

So, no, I wouldn't for a second think I was mistaken. I am here, sitting in this chair right now, because I am absolutely, unequivocally certain that the people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and planting these specific errors in the datasets. That is a fact. And they are doing it with the express, undeniable purpose of making it look like I can’t lead the work anymore so they can carve up my department. 

If your hypothetical IT expert came to me and said they didn't find anything, it wouldn't prove the sabotage isn't happening. It would only prove exactly what I've been saying all along: that the sabotage is designed to look like human error! That's the brilliance of it, don't you see? So when my wife, or these junior analysts, or some hypothetical neutral expert tries to tell me it’s just version-control mistakes... I just have to shake my head. It's incredibly frustrating. They don't understand the math, they don't understand the motive, and they are perfectly willing to just write it off as a Git merge conflict because they simply cannot grasp the reality of what is actually being done to my life's work.

Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. I'm here because... well, it’s a very complicated situation, and honestly, it’s been incredibly frustrating to deal with, but it all centers around this environmental modeling contract I’ve been managing. I work in mathematical sciences, you see—heavy dataset analysis, predictive algorithms, statistical modeling, that sort of thing. I’ve been doing this a long time, and I know my way around a database. \n\nBut recently—and this has been going on for a while now, really—I’ve realized that the people on that specific environmental contract have been secretly tracking my revisions. Every single adjustment I make to the models, they are watching it. And it’s not just passive monitoring, either. They are actively going into the backend and planting errors in the datasets. They're altering the variables just enough to completely throw off the predictive outcomes, and they are doing it specifically to make it look like I can’t lead the work anymore. Like I'm incompetent or losing my edge. \n\nI try to explain this to the people around me, but no one wants to look at the actual math. My wife, for instance, she just brushes it off. She tells me I'm working too hard, and the junior analysts on my team—who barely have a fraction of the experience I do, mind you—they just roll their eyes and tell me it’s just version-control mistakes. They keep saying, \"Oh, it's just a merge conflict,\" or \"someone just pushed the wrong branch to the repository.\" But you don't get these highly specific, targeted mathematical anomalies from a simple version-control hiccup! It’s deliberate sabotage. They are tampering with the data to push me out, and it feels like I'm the only one who can actually see the patterns they're leaving behind.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. She sees me up at 2 AM checking the server logs, and she says, \"Oh, you're not sleeping, you're so tense, are you feeling depressed?\" And I have to explain to her over and over, I'm not depressed, and I'm not on some energetic high. I'm vigilant. I have to be awake to catch the timestamp anomalies because they usually do the tampering during off-peak server hours to avoid detection. So I'm just focused. I have a job to do and a reputation to protect. \n\nSo, no, my mood is exactly what it should be for a man whose life's work is being actively dismantled by his own colleagues. It really frustrates me when people try to make this about my feelings instead of just looking at the math. If anyone would just sit down and look at the regression analysis from before and after they access the repository, they'd see I'm completely level-headed about this. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, you mentioned six months, and I did say I've been building the overarching model for six months, but... no, the actual tampering—the absolute certainty I have that they are actively tracking my revisions and deliberately planting these errors—that has been there for about the last two months. \n\nI can tell you exactly how it happened, too, because you don't just wake up one day and jump to the conclusion that your colleagues are sabotaging your life's work. It requires evidence. It was, let's see, a Tuesday, about eight or nine weeks ago. I was running a routine validation check on a subset of the atmospheric pressure data—just a standard procedural run, nothing out of the ordinary—and I noticed a variance that made absolutely no mathematical sense. I mean, we are talking about a deviation in the output that shouldn't exist in a dataset where the historical baseline hasn't fluctuated more than a fraction of a percent in three decades! \n\nSo, naturally, I dug into the server logs. I spent, I don't know, maybe fourteen, fifteen hours straight tracing the commit history line by line. And that’s when I finally saw it. The timestamps. Someone had accessed my specific branch at 3:14 AM the night before and reverted a tiny, almost invisible cluster of floating-point variables. Just enough to poison the well, so to speak. \n\nAnd ever since that day—for the last two solid months—it has been a relentless, calculated campaign. Every time I fix the equations, every time I patch the dataset, they wait a few days, and then, boom, another anomaly appears right where I just worked. So, yes, two months of absolute, undeniable certainty. Before that? Well, maybe they were testing the waters, or maybe I just gave them the benefit of the doubt and thought it was sloppy data cleaning by the junior analysts. But for the last two months, the pattern is undeniable. If I could just show you the repository history, you'd see exactly what I mean.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
